I can't even force myself to take a bite most days, my stomach just sort of rejects the idea of it. So I just end up throwing most of it away in the trash can by the time clock before I have to clock back in and go back out to the sales floor. It's just a complete waste of money and food, but I just have zero appetite, and it's been happening every single day, which is why I figured I should probably come in and talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... down, I guess, but mostly it's just like... I don't really care about anything anymore. I used to have this whole routine, you know? Like, outside of the store. Because retail just completely drains you, so you gotta have things to look forward to. \n\nI used to get super pumped about going to these gallery nights downtown. They do this thing on the first Friday of every month where all the little local shops and art studios open their doors, and it's just a really cool vibe. I'd go with some buddies, and we'd always hit up the food trucks parked by the square—grabbing these amazing al pastor tacos from this one specific truck that's only ever there on weekends. Or, you know, playing pickup basketball at the rec center on Tuesday nights. I even used to volunteer sometimes, helping out the younger kids at the community center with their art stuff, like painting and drawing, since I've always kind of messed around with sketching and I liked seeing them get creative. \n\nBut lately? Man, I just... I don't even know. My friends will try to drag me out to the courts, or we'll go to the gallery thing, and I just stand there. I'm just standing there on the sidewalk with a taco in my hand, or standing on the edge of the basketball court while everyone else is shooting around, and I just feel completely flat. Like, nothing. There's no spark, no excitement, no nothing. It's like my brain is just totally blank to it. So I usually just end up making some dumb excuse to leave early. I'll tell them I have an opening shift the next day and need to sleep, or that my stomach is acting up—which I guess isn't a total lie since I'm not eating anyway—but really I just want to get out of there. It's just exhausting trying to pretend I'm having fun when I'm just feeling so empty about all of it, you know? It's like the color got sucked out of everything I used to like doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Um, I mean, I guess I wouldn't say I feel sad or depressed all day, every day. Like, it's not this constant dark cloud or anything like that where I'm just sitting around crying. It's more just... I don't know, like I was saying before, it's just this emptiness. But as for actually feeling down or depressed most of the day, for two weeks straight? No, I don't think it's been like that. \n\nI mean, I still go to work, right? I still clock in at the store. And when I'm on the floor, I'm just kind of going through the motions. I'll be stocking the shelves in the home goods section, making sure all the towels are folded right and the candles are faced forward, and I'm not really feeling sad while I'm doing it. I'm just... doing it. And then I'll have to deal with a customer who's upset about a return policy or something, and I just sort of nod and apologize and call a manager over, and I don't feel depressed then either. I just feel completely detached from the whole interaction. \n\nSo it's not like I'm walking around the store feeling miserable and depressed all day. It's just that when I have a second to think, or when I'm off the clock, that's when I notice that I just don't care about anything. But I wouldn't call it being depressed most of the day, every day. It's more just a lack of feeling anything at all, if that makes sense. I don't know if that's the same thing as being depressed, but it definitely doesn't feel like sadness. It just feels like... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, even if it wasn’t severe?\n\nPatient: Um, well, like I was trying to explain, it's not really like that. I wouldn't say I've felt down or depressed most of the time on any particular day, honestly. It's just... it's hard to explain because I know people think \"depression\" and they think crying in bed all day, and I'm not doing that. \n\nLike yesterday, for example. I had the opening shift, so I was up at 4:30 AM, taking the bus in the dark. And usually, taking the early bus, you see the same people, right? I didn't turn on the TV, didn't look at my phone. I just sat there until it got dark. But I wasn't sitting there feeling sad or down or miserable. I wasn't dwelling on anything bad. It's just this total absence of feeling. So, no, I really haven't had days where I felt down or depressed most of the time, not even mildly. It's just this constant flatline, you know? I mean, the energy thing is just... it's huge right now. And it doesn't even seem to matter if I sleep, you know? Like the battery is pulled out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a big drop in your energy level or feel more tired than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, the energy thing is just... it's huge right now. And it doesn't even seem to matter if I sleep, you know? I could be in bed for eight or nine hours, just laying there or sleeping or whatever, and I'll still wake up feeling like I just ran a marathon in my sleep. But work is where I really, really notice it. \n\nI mean, retail is always tiring anyway because you're on your feet all day on that cheap linoleum over concrete, and that messes with your knees and your back. But this is completely different. It's like... by the middle of a shift, my whole body just feels so incredibly heavy. Like my arms and legs are made out of wet cement or lead or something. \n\nI'll be out on the floor, right? And part of my job is just basic maintenance, making sure the store looks presentable for the regional managers or whoever. So I'll be over in the apparel section, and I have to straighten the clothing racks. Usually, you just breeze through it, you know? You just push the hangers back, space them out, make sure the sizes are in order, pick up the shirts people dropped on the floor. It's mindless. But lately, just lifting my arm to push a row of hangers feels like this massive, impossible chore. It takes way, way more out of me than it ever should. \n\nAnd going to the back? Man, forget about it. If a customer asks me to check if we have a different size in the back, I used to just jog back to the stockroom, check the rolling bins, and come right back out in two minutes. Now, just the thought of walking all the way across the store, pushing through those swinging double doors, and walking into the stockroom... it's exhausting just thinking about it. I have to physically drag myself back there. Every single step feels like I'm walking through deep mud. It's this deep, heavy exhaustion that just settles into my bones by like, noon, and I'll just find myself leaning against the endcaps or the register counter because just holding my own body upright takes too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re worthless or felt guilty about things more than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, actually, I have. It's, um, it's been really messing with my head lately, especially at work. \n\nA couple of weeks ago, right around when things started feeling really off, my store manager pulled me into the back office. And usually, you know, when they call you back there, it's because somebody messed up a promotional display or a register was short at the end of the night, right? So I was already kind of bracing myself to get chewed out. But instead, she told me I was getting this \"Team Member of the Quarter\" award. They even gave me this little plastic pin for my lanyard and a twenty-dollar gift card to a steakhouse. And on top of that, she said the district manager was looking at me for an open shift supervisor position that's coming up next month. \n\nI know I should be happy about it, or at least care, but the thing is... I just feel sick to my stomach about the whole thing. I keep thinking I absolutely do not deserve that award. Like, at all. And I definitely don't deserve a chance at being a supervisor. I mean, I don't even have my high school diploma, and I can barely drag my own body to the stockroom right now, so how am I supposed to run a whole shift and tell other people what to do? \n\nAnd it's not just feeling worthless about it, it's this... this massive, crushing guilt. I feel like I've somehow tricked all of them. Like I've been running this huge scam, making them think I'm this great, reliable worker, and I'm actually just a total fraud. I even tried to talk to my manager about it the other day. I went up to her and said, you know, maybe they should give the supervisor spot to Marcus in electronics, because he's better with the customers and I don't think I'm right for it. But she just brushed it off. People keep telling me I earned it, that I'm a hard worker and I didn't trick anyone, but I just can't shake the feeling. I go in there every single day feeling like a criminal who's getting away with something, just waiting for everyone to figure out that I'm completely useless. It's just... it's exhausting carrying that around on top of how heavy my body already feels.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything you do, including activities you used to enjoy, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it is. It's like I was saying before about feeling blank at work, it just bleeds into everything else I do, too. Stuff I used to really look forward to, it just doesn't do anything for me anymore. Not even for a second. \n\nLike, I used to be huge into playing basketball. We had this regular pickup game at the park down the street, usually Tuesdays and Thursdays if the weather was good. I'd get my gear ready the night before, text the guys, get all hyped up to run some games. But I tried going a couple of weeks ago, and I was just standing there on the court while everyone else was running around, calling out plays and sweating. I just felt completely flat. I didn't care who had the ball, didn't care about the score. I just stood there for like ten minutes, faked a pulled hamstring, and made an excuse to leave. \n\nAnd it’s the same with going out. There’s this spot downtown where all the food trucks park on the weekends, and I used to get so excited to go down there and grab these amazing al pastor tacos with the pineapple on top. I’d crave them all week. My buddy dragged me down there recently, and I was just standing in line, looking at the trucks and smelling the food, and I just didn't care. I felt absolutely nothing. I was on the register just the other day—it was Tuesday, I think, because that’s when the new apparel shipment comes in and the store is always an absolute mess with cardboard boxes everywhere—and I was ringing people up just as fast as I always do. My hands were moving fine, scanning the barcodes, folding the shirts, bagging everything up, doing the whole customer service routine. I can still remember all the PLU codes for the random items that are missing their price tags, and I can still do the math for the change in my head if the register computer freezes up, which it does all the time because our system is ancient. So my brain is definitely working at regular speed. \n\nIt’s just that physically, my body feels incredibly heavy, but I'm still forcing myself to push through it at a normal pace. I'm not, like, stuck in slow-mo or taking ten minutes to process a thought or answer a question. I actually kind of wish my brain would slow down sometimes, honestly, just so I wouldn't have to think so much about the promotion and the award and all of that. But yeah, no, the actual speed of my thinking and moving hasn't really changed.\n\nDoctor: When did these symptoms first start? Have they been going on for at least two weeks, or have they been more long-standing?\n\nPatient: It's been... yeah, definitely way more than two weeks. It's been about two months now, actually. \n\nIt all really started right around the time I got that window display award, which is what led to all that other stuff I was telling you about earlier. It was, I don't know, maybe eight or nine weeks ago? Corporate had sent down this massive new visual merchandising directive for the front windows for the season change. And usually, my store manager just has us slap up the standard promotional posters and dress the mannequins in whatever the featured denim is for the week, nothing crazy. But we were really short-staffed that Tuesday, and she just told me to handle the whole front-of-house setup by myself. \n\nSo I spent, like, three hours dragging all these heavy props out of the basement, setting up this whole layered look with the fake autumn leaves they sent us and the new outerwear line. I didn't even think it was that special, honestly. I was just trying to get it done as fast as possible so I could finally go on my lunch break because I was starving. But then the district manager did a surprise walk-through the very next morning. And he made this huge, massive deal out of it. He was taking pictures of my window on his iPad, saying he was sending it to the regional team, and suddenly I'm getting all this intense attention from him. So yeah, it’s been two solid months of this. Two months of feeling like a complete fraud every single time I walk past that front window, just waiting for the district manager to realize he made a huge mistake. Like, I'll be standing there scanning a huge stack of clearance jeans, and my mind is just firing off all these reasons why I'm a fraud and why I shouldn't get that supervisor job, just rapid-fire, over and over. I can still remember all the PLU codes for the random items that are missing their price tags, and I can still do the math for the change in my head if the register computer freezes up, which it does all the time because our system is ancient. So my brain is definitely working at regular speed. \n\nIt’s just that physically, my body feels incredibly heavy, but I'm still forcing myself to push through it at a normal pace. I'm not, like, stuck in slow-mo or taking ten minutes to process a thought or answer a question. I actually kind of wish my brain would slow down sometimes, honestly, just so I wouldn't have to think so much about the promotion and the award and all of that. But yeah, no, the actual speed of my thinking and moving hasn't really changed.\n\nDoctor: When did these symptoms first start? Have they been going on for at least two weeks, or have they been more long-standing?\n\nPatient: It's been... yeah, definitely way more than two weeks. It's been about two months now, actually. \n\nIt all really started right around the time I got that window display award, which is what led to all that other stuff I was telling you about earlier. It was, I don't know, maybe eight or nine weeks ago? Corporate had sent down this massive new visual merchandising directive for the front windows for the season change. And usually, my store manager just has us slap up the standard promotional posters and dress the mannequins in whatever the featured denim is for the week, nothing crazy. But we were really short-staffed that Tuesday, and she just told me to handle the whole front-of-house setup by myself. \n\nSo I spent, like, three hours dragging all these heavy props out of the basement, setting up this whole layered look with the fake autumn leaves they sent us and the new outerwear line. I didn't even think it was that special, honestly. I was just trying to get it done as fast as possible so I could finally go on my lunch break because I was starving. But then the district manager did a surprise walk-through the very next morning. And he made this huge, massive deal out of it. He was taking pictures of my window on his iPad, saying he was sending it to the regional team, and suddenly I'm getting all this intense attention from him. He pulled me aside and started asking me about my long-term career goals, which is when the whole supervisor track thing started. \n\nAnd ever since that exact week, about two months ago... that's when all of this just started crushing me. Because everyone is acting like I'm some kind of retail genius who put all this brilliant thought into a window display, and I literally just threw some fake leaves around some jackets because I wanted to go to the food court and get a pretzel. So yeah, it’s been two solid months of this. Two months of feeling like a complete fraud every single time I walk past that front window, just waiting for the district manager to realize he made a huge mistake. It feels like it's been a year, honestly, just dragging myself in there day after day since that visit...\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything you do, including activities you used to enjoy, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um... I think I actually just went over this a few minutes ago, right? But yeah, the answer is definitely yes. It's exactly like I was saying before about all the stuff I used to really get into. \n\nI used to get so incredibly excited about those local gallery nights on the first Fridays, or getting my gear together to play pickup basketball with the guys at the park, or even just going downtown and grabbing those al pastor food-truck tacos I was telling you about. And especially helping the neighborhood kids with their art projects at the community center. I mean, that stuff used to be the absolute highlight of my week, you know? I'd look forward to it the whole time I was stuck at the store dealing with customers and the messy clearance racks. \n\nBut lately... it's just completely gone. It's exactly like I was describing. I just show up to these things and stand there feeling totally flat. It doesn't matter if it's a really close basketball game or a kid showing me a painting they're super proud of, I just feel absolutely nothing. Not even a quick spark of it. I usually just end up making some kind of excuse to leave, like I told you about faking that pulled hamstring on the court, or making up that errand so I could walk away from the taco line, or saying I had a migraine at the art show. It just feels exhausting and pointless to stay when I'm just a blank wall inside. So, yeah, it's everything. Absolutely everything I used to enjoy is just... it's just dead to me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt this kind of emotional numbness, loss of interest, and guilt lasting weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had times where I was bummed out, you know? Like back in high school, before I dropped out, I'd get really stressed about grades or just feel kind of down for a few days if something bad happened, like if a girl I liked didn't like me back or if I got in a fight with my mom. But that was always, like, a normal kind of sad. It made sense. And it would go away. I'd go play some basketball or hang out with my friends and I'd bounce right back. \n\nBut this... this is completely different. I've never had anything even close to this before. This isn't just being sad for a weekend. This is like my whole brain has been rewired or something. I've never gone weeks and weeks feeling this completely empty, where I can't even force myself to care about a pickup game or the neighborhood kids. And I've definitely never had this intense, crushing guilt before, where I feel like I'm tricking everyone around me just by existing. Like I told you, this all started two months ago with that stupid window display. Before that, I was just a regular guy working a regular retail job, trying to figure things out. I've never been stuck in this kind of... whatever this is... for this long. It's completely new, and it's terrifying, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a depressed mood or low mood for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Two years? No, no, definitely not two years. Like I was just saying, it's really just been these last two months. I mean, if this had been going on for two years, I don't even know if I'd still be here, honestly. The thought of feeling this... this heavy and empty for two whole years? Just dragging myself out of bed, feeling like a total fraud, going to the store, pretending to be this great employee that the district manager thinks I am, and then coming home and just staring at the wall... doing that for seven hundred and thirty days? I can't even imagine. \n\nTwo years ago, I was actually doing okay. I mean, I was still working at the store, obviously, but I was just a regular sales associate. I didn't have any of this pressure. I was twenty-three, just hanging out, playing basketball, eating tacos, not really worrying about anything too serious. I wasn't supervisor material, and I was fine with that. I wasn't walking around feeling like I was deceiving everyone. I'd go to work, fold the sweaters, ring people up, and then clock out and leave it all behind. I'd actually look forward to the weekend. I'd look forward to the gallery nights. \n\nSo, no, it hasn't been two years. Not even close. It's just been since that Tuesday. That specific Tuesday two months ago when we were short-staffed and I had to do the window display by myself because Sarah called out sick and the manager was stressed. That's the exact starting point. Before that, my mood was totally fine. I mean, normal fine, you know? Not amazing every day, but definitely not this constant, crushing low that I've been stuck in for the last eight weeks. So yeah, definitely not two years. Just two months. But honestly, these two months have felt longer than the two years before them combined.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood is worse in the morning, or that you feel particularly slowed down or physically heavy early in the day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like the mornings are specifically worse than the afternoons or the evenings, you know? It's just... it's all the same heavy feeling. It doesn't matter if my alarm is going off at 7:00 AM so I can get to the store and open the registers, or if it's 3:00 PM and I'm on my lunch break eating a sad sandwich in the back room, or if it's 11:00 PM and I'm just lying in bed staring at the ceiling fan. It's just this constant, flat, empty weight that never really shifts or changes based on what the clock says. \n\nI guess you could say waking up is hard, but not because I feel physically heavier then. It's just hard because I have to open my eyes and realize that I have to do it all over again. I have to go back to the store, I have to walk past that window display, I have to smile at the customers and pretend I deserve the supervisor badge they gave me. So the thought of getting out of bed is exhausting, sure, but once I'm actually up and moving, I don't feel any more slowed down than I do at, like, 4:00 in the afternoon when I'm trying to fold a stack of jeans and my arms just feel like lead. \n\nSo, yeah, it's not a morning thing. It's an all-day thing. From the second I wake up to the second I finally manage to fall asleep, it's just one long, continuous stretch of feeling like this fraud who's just going through the motions. There's no dip in the morning and no relief at night. It's just... always there. Just a constant, heavy blanket over everything, no matter what time it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything you do, including activities you used to enjoy, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... absolutely everything. It's like someone just flipped a switch and drained all the color out of the world, you know? It's not just the big things, it's the little things too. Like, I used to really look forward to the first Friday of every month. That's when they do the gallery nights downtown, right? And I'm no artist or anything, obviously, I just work at the store, but I always loved going down there. I'd walk around, look at the paintings, talk to the people selling their stuff on the sidewalk, maybe grab some food-truck tacos—they have this one truck that makes these amazing al pastor tacos with pineapple on top, and I used to crave them all month. I'd go with a couple of guys from the neighborhood, or sometimes just by myself, and it was just... it was fun. It felt good. \n\nBut the last time I went, which was maybe three weeks ago, I just... I felt nothing. I was standing in front of this really cool abstract painting that the old me would have stared at for twenty minutes, and I just felt completely flat. The tacos tasted like cardboard. I couldn't even pretend to care. I ended up making some excuse about having a headache and just went home early. \n\nAnd it's the same with basketball. I've been playing pickup games at the park down the street since I was, like, fifteen. Every Sunday morning, rain or shine. It was my favorite part of the week. But now? The guys text me to come down, and I just stare at my phone. I went once, a couple weeks after the whole window display thing happened, and I was just going through the motions. Running up and down the court, passing the ball, but I wasn't really there. I didn't care if we won or lost, I didn't care about making a good shot. I just felt empty. I faked a pulled hamstring after twenty minutes so I could leave. \n\nEven the kids in my building... there are these two little kids, maybe seven or eight, who live down the hall, and sometimes they sit in the hallway drawing with chalk or crayons, and I used to stop and help them, you know? Draw a dinosaur or a spaceship or whatever, just goofing around. They're good kids. But last week, I walked past them, and they asked me to draw a dragon, and I just... I couldn't do it. I just looked at them and felt this horrible, heavy blankness. I told them I was late for work, even though it was my day off, and I just went into my apartment and locked the door. So, yeah. Everything. Nothing feels good anymore. Nothing even feels okay. It's just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this current two-month stretch where you felt persistently down, lost interest in things, or felt emotionally numb for weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, never. I mean, like I was saying, it really just started with that Tuesday two months ago. Before that? Nothing like this. \n\nI mean, look, it’s not like my life has been some perfect fairy tale or anything. I dropped out of high school back in the 11th grade, which... you know, that was a whole big mess. My mom was really upset, and I felt pretty bad about letting her down. I was stressed out because I had to find a full-time job and figure things out, but... but it wasn't *this*. Even when I was stressed back then, I still wanted to hang out with my friends. I still cared about things. I didn't feel numb. I just felt like a regular teenager who messed up and needed to get his act together. \n\nAnd since then, working retail all these years, you definitely have bad days. You get customers yelling at you over a return policy for a sweater they clearly wore and spilled coffee on, or the manager schedules you for a 'clopening' shift—where you close the store at 11 PM and have to be back to open at 7 AM—and yeah, you feel tired. You feel grumpy. I’d complain to the guys at the park, or I’d be in a bad mood for a day or two. But it always passed. I’d get a good night's sleep, go grab some tacos, and I’d be fine again. \n\nI never had weeks and weeks of just... nothing. I never had a time where I felt completely empty and disconnected from everything for weeks at a time. Up until two months ago, I was just a normal guy. I went to work, I did my shifts, I came home, and I felt normal. So, no, I've never had a period like this before in my entire life. This is completely new, and I guess that's why it's so... it's just so exhausting, because I don't even know how to handle it since I've never had to deal with anything even close to this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two years in a row?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, wow. No, definitely not. Like I was saying just a minute ago, this all started exactly two months ago. Before that, I was totally fine. I mean, two whole years of feeling like this... I honestly don't think I could survive that, you know? Just thinking about carrying this... this heavy, empty feeling around for twenty-four months straight, it just makes my chest hurt even thinking about it. \n\nIf you look at my life two years ago, I was, what, twenty-three? That was right around the time our old store manager, Dave, transferred to the bigger location out in the suburbs, and things were actually going pretty well for me. I was working my normal shifts, hanging out at the park playing basketball, going to the gallery nights downtown... I was just living my life. Sure, I had days where I was annoyed because my paycheck seemed a little light after taxes, or I had to deal with a rude customer, but I never felt *down* like this. I definitely didn't lose interest in anything. I was always the first one on the court on Sunday mornings. I loved my life, even if it was just a simple one.\n\nSo, yeah, definitely not two years. Not even two months and one week. It's just been this very specific, very sudden thing that hit me two months ago on that Tuesday. Before that, my life was just... regular. Just a regular guy working retail, paying rent, eating tacos. Nothing like this two-year thing you're talking about. I can't even wrap my head around feeling this way for that long. Like, how does anyone even get out of bed if it goes on for years? Because right now, two months feels like an absolute eternity to me. I just... I really hope this doesn't last for two years. That's a terrifying thought, honestly."
